Well, I guess what finally made me come in is this thing with the historical society. I sort of… took over the fundraiser, or tried to. I was acting like I could run the whole thing myself—making quilt designs, calling all these volunteers, drawing up schedules, promising people deadlines. I used to help with sewing projects years ago, my aunt had this old quilting frame in her dining room, and I kept thinking, “Oh, I know how this should go.” But I got carried away. I just kept adding more and more, and now I’ve made a bit of a mess of it.

Yes, I think so… this fundraiser is just the clearest example because it’s all out in public now. I started with just helping a little, and then suddenly I was doing quilt patterns, calling people, making schedules, telling everyone when things would be done—like I was in charge of the whole thing.

I’ve had that sort of thing happen before, where I get very sure I can handle everything. It reminds me of my aunt’s quilting frame, actually—she could keep a whole room of women organized without even raising her voice. I suppose I thought I could do that too. But then I promise too much, and it turns into more than I can really manage.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… yes, I suppose it does, though the historical society one is the one everyone can point to. I started out just helping, and then I was making quilt designs, calling volunteers, writing schedules, promising deadlines—like I could run the whole fundraiser myself.

And quilting is funny, you know, because once you start laying out the pieces, you think, “Oh, I can see the whole pattern,” but then there are all these little seams and corners you didn’t account for. That’s sort of how I get. I see the big plan and jump in, and then suddenly I’ve said yes to far more than I can actually finish.

It reminds me of my aunt’s quilting frame, actually—she could keep a whole room of women organized without even raising her voice. I suppose I thought I could do that too. But then I promise too much, and it turns into more than I can really manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this pattern of taking on too much and then struggling is something that happens in other areas of your life, not just with the historical society?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose it does, though the historical society one is the one everyone can point to. I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I do like things to look right, especially with sewing—my aunt always said if your corners don’t meet, the whole quilt tells on you. But I don’t know that I think something terrible will happen.

It’s more like… I get caught up in how it ought to be, and then I start fussing over the design or the schedule, and before I know it I’m talking about fabric choices when someone only asked me what time the meeting starts. So perfection, maybe, but not in a “bad thing will happen” way. More like I lose the thread a bit.

I don’t know if it’s being in charge exactly. I don’t sit there thinking, “I have to run this or I won’t be comfortable.” It’s more that if nobody seems to know what’s happening, I start filling in the blanks.

Like with a sewing circle, if the thread and scissors and patterns are all scattered around, someone has to put them in order—or at least that’s what my mind does. My aunt used to have little tins for every color of button, and I always admired that. But then I suppose I can get carried away and start deciding where everyone’s buttons ought to go, even if they didn’t ask me.

So… maybe I feel better when there’s a plan. I’m not sure I’d say I need to be in charge, though.

I was acting like I could run the whole thing myself—making quilt designs, calling all these volunteers, drawing up schedules, promising people deadlines. And then, of course, it was way more than one person could do.\n\nI used to help with sewing projects years ago, my aunt had this old quilting frame in her dining room, and I kept thinking, “Oh, I know how this should go.” But I got carried away. I just kept adding more and more, and now I’ve made a bit of a mess of it.\n\nDoctor: Has this kind of thing, where you take on too much and then struggle, happened to you before?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… this fundraiser is just the clearest example because it’s all out in public now. I started with just helping a little, and then suddenly I was doing quilt patterns, calling people, making schedules, telling everyone when things would be done—like I was in charge of the whole thing.\n\nI’ve had that sort of thing happen before, where I get very sure I can handle everything. It reminds me of my aunt’s quilting frame, actually—she could keep a whole room of women organized without even raising her voice. I suppose I thought I could do that too. But then I promise too much, and it turns into more than I can really manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this pattern of taking on too much and then struggling is something that happens in other areas of your life, not just with the historical society?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose it does, though the historical society one is the one everyone can point to. I started out just helping, and then I was making quilt designs, calling volunteers, writing schedules, promising deadlines—like I could run the whole fundraiser myself.\n\nAnd quilting is funny, you know, because once you start laying out the pieces, you think, “Oh, I can see the whole pattern,” but then there are all these little seams and corners you didn’t account for. That’s sort of how I get. I see the big plan and jump in, and then suddenly I’ve said yes to far more than I can actually finish.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to do things perfectly, or that if you don't, something bad will happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I do like things to look right, especially with sewing—my aunt always said if your corners don’t meet, the whole quilt tells on you. But I don’t know that I think something terrible will happen.\n\nIt’s more like… I get caught up in how it ought to be, and then I start fussing over the design or the schedule, and before I know it I’m talking about fabric choices when someone only asked me what time the meeting starts. So perfection, maybe, but not in a “bad thing will happen” way. More like I lose the thread a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to be in charge of situations to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s being in charge exactly. I don’t sit there thinking, “I have to run this or I won’t be comfortable.” It’s more that if nobody seems to know what’s happening, I start filling in the blanks.\n\nLike with a sewing circle, if the thread and scissors and patterns are all scattered around, someone has to put them in order—or at least that’s what my mind does. My aunt used to have little tins for every color of button, and I always admired that. But then I suppose I can get carried away and start deciding where everyone’s buttons ought to go, even if they didn’t ask me.\n\nSo… maybe I feel better when there’s a plan. I’m not sure I’d say I need to be in charge, though.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yes… I mean, I suppose it is causing problems. More than I like to admit.

My husband has had to pick up a lot of the house routines lately—things I always used to keep track of, like what needs doing and who’s supposed to be where, that sort of thing. And with the historical society… well, he’s had to deal with some of that too, because I stopped keeping up with what I’d promised. I just couldn’t face people waiting on me, asking where things were.

It’s embarrassing, really. I start out thinking I’m helping, and then it turns into this big tangled spool of thread, and suddenly I don’t want to answer the phone.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what finally made me come in is this thing with the historical society. I sort of… took over the fundraiser, or tried to. And then, of course, it was way more than one person could do.\n\nI used to help with sewing projects years ago, my aunt had this old quilting frame in her dining room, and I kept thinking, “Oh, I know how this should go.” But I got carried away. I just kept adding more and more, and now I’ve made a bit of a mess of it.\n\nDoctor: Has this kind of thing, where you take on too much and then struggle, happened to you before?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… this fundraiser is just the clearest example because it’s all out in public now. I started with just helping a little, and then suddenly I was doing quilt patterns, calling people, making schedules, telling everyone when things would be done—like I was in charge of the whole thing.\n\nI’ve had that sort of thing happen before, where I get very sure I can handle everything. It reminds me of my aunt’s quilting frame, actually—she could keep a whole room of women organized without even raising her voice. I suppose I thought I could do that too. But then I promise too much, and it turns into more than I can really manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this pattern of taking on too much and then struggling is something that happens in other areas of your life, not just with the historical society?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose it does, though the historical society one is the one everyone can point to. I started out just helping, and then I was making quilt designs, calling volunteers, writing schedules, promising deadlines—like I could run the whole fundraiser myself.\n\nAnd quilting is funny, you know, because once you start laying out the pieces, you think, “Oh, I can see the whole pattern,” but then there are all these little seams and corners you didn’t account for. That’s sort of how I get. I see the big plan and jump in, and then suddenly I’ve said yes to far more than I can actually finish.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to do things perfectly, or that if you don't, something bad will happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I do like things to look right, especially with sewing—my aunt always said if your corners don’t meet, the whole quilt tells on you. But I don’t know that I think something terrible will happen.\n\nIt’s more like… I get caught up in how it ought to be, and then I start fussing over the design or the schedule, and before I know it I’m talking about fabric choices when someone only asked me what time the meeting starts. So perfection, maybe, but not in a “bad thing will happen” way. More like I lose the thread a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to be in charge of situations to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s being in charge exactly. My aunt used to have little tins for every color of button, and I always admired that. I’m not sure I’d say I need to be in charge, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your tendency to take on too much and then struggle is causing you a lot of stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I suppose it is causing problems. And with the historical society… well, he’s had to deal with some of that too, because I stopped keeping up with what I’d promised. I just couldn’t face people waiting on me, asking where things were.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, really. I’m not sure… I mean, I worry about things, but I don’t know if it’s more than anybody else would. There’s always something to think about, isn’t there? Bills, family, whether I remembered to put the good scissors back where they belong—my aunt had a pair she’d practically guard with her life.

But anxious… I don’t know. Sometimes I get wound up when things feel unfinished, or when I think I’ve left someone waiting. Then again, I can spend half an hour fretting over a pattern piece and forget what I was upset about in the first place. So yes, maybe some worry, but I’m not sure how to measure “often.”

I’m not sure I’d say I need to be in charge, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your tendency to take on too much and then struggle is causing you a lot of stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I suppose it is causing problems. I just couldn’t face people waiting on me, asking where things were.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, really. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I do get little ideas stuck in my head sometimes, like “I should sort that drawer” or “I need to call so-and-so,” but I don’t know that I feel compelled exactly.

It’s funny, because my aunt used to say if a thought keeps tapping at the window, you might as well let it in—but she was talking about quilting patterns, not anything serious. Sometimes I’ll start with one thought and then it turns into three other things, and before I know it I’m standing in the pantry wondering why I came in there.

So… recurring thoughts, maybe in the ordinary sense. But feeling forced to act on them? I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed anything like that in a clear way.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what finally made me come in is this thing with the historical society. I sort of… took over the fundraiser, or tried to. I was acting like I could run the whole thing myself—making quilt designs, calling all these volunteers, drawing up schedules, promising people deadlines. And then, of course, it was way more than one person could do.\n\nI used to help with sewing projects years ago, my aunt had this old quilting frame in her dining room, and I kept thinking, “Oh, I know how this should go.” But I got carried away. I just kept adding more and more, and now I’ve made a bit of a mess of it.\n\nDoctor: Has this kind of thing, where you take on too much and then struggle, happened to you before?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… this fundraiser is just the clearest example because it’s all out in public now. I started with just helping a little, and then suddenly I was doing quilt patterns, calling people, making schedules, telling everyone when things would be done—like I was in charge of the whole thing.\n\nI’ve had that sort of thing happen before, where I get very sure I can handle everything. It reminds me of my aunt’s quilting frame, actually—she could keep a whole room of women organized without even raising her voice. I suppose I thought I could do that too. But then I promise too much, and it turns into more than I can really manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this pattern of taking on too much and then struggling is something that happens in other areas of your life, not just with the historical society?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose it does, though the historical society one is the one everyone can point to. I started out just helping, and then I was making quilt designs, calling volunteers, writing schedules, promising deadlines—like I could run the whole fundraiser myself.\n\nAnd quilting is funny, you know, because once you start laying out the pieces, you think, “Oh, I can see the whole pattern,” but then there are all these little seams and corners you didn’t account for. That’s sort of how I get. I see the big plan and jump in, and then suddenly I’ve said yes to far more than I can actually finish.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to do things perfectly, or that if you don't, something bad will happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I do like things to look right, especially with sewing—my aunt always said if your corners don’t meet, the whole quilt tells on you. But I don’t know that I think something terrible will happen.\n\nIt’s more like… I get caught up in how it ought to be, and then I start fussing over the design or the schedule, and before I know it I’m talking about fabric choices when someone only asked me what time the meeting starts. So perfection, maybe, but not in a “bad thing will happen” way. More like I lose the thread a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to be in charge of situations to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s being in charge exactly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know… I’m not sure it’s either of those exactly. Perfect, control—those both sound a bit stronger than how it feels in my head.

I think sometimes I just get carried along by the idea of doing something useful, or not disappointing someone. And then, well, once you’ve said yes, it’s like when you start pulling one thread and the whole hem comes loose. My mother used to say you should never agree to alter a dress before you’ve looked at the seams properly, and I suppose that applies to other things too.

So… I don’t know if it’s perfection or control. I haven’t really thought of it that way. It just feels like I mean well, and then I’ve made a mess of too many promises.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF60.8, F34.0, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what finally made me come in is this thing with the historical society. I sort of… took over the fundraiser, or tried to. I was acting like I could run the whole thing myself—making quilt designs, calling all these volunteers, drawing up schedules, promising people deadlines. And then, of course, it was way more than one person could do.\n\nI used to help with sewing projects years ago, my aunt had this old quilting frame in her dining room, and I kept thinking, “Oh, I know how this should go.” But I got carried away. I just kept adding more and more, and now I’ve made a bit of a mess of it.\n\nDoctor: Has this kind of thing, where you take on too much and then struggle, happened to you before?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… this fundraiser is just the clearest example because it’s all out in public now. I started with just helping a little, and then suddenly I was doing quilt patterns, calling people, making schedules, telling everyone when things would be done—like I was in charge of the whole thing.\n\nI’ve had that sort of thing happen before, where I get very sure I can handle everything. It reminds me of my aunt’s quilting frame, actually—she could keep a whole room of women organized without even raising her voice. I suppose I thought I could do that too. But then I promise too much, and it turns into more than I can really manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this pattern of taking on too much and then struggling is something that happens in other areas of your life, not just with the historical society?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose it does, though the historical society one is the one everyone can point to. I started out just helping, and then I was making quilt designs, calling volunteers, writing schedules, promising deadlines—like I could run the whole fundraiser myself.\n\nAnd quilting is funny, you know, because once you start laying out the pieces, you think, “Oh, I can see the whole pattern,” but then there are all these little seams and corners you didn’t account for. That’s sort of how I get. I see the big plan and jump in, and then suddenly I’ve said yes to far more than I can actually finish.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to do things perfectly, or that if you don't, something bad will happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I do like things to look right, especially with sewing—my aunt always said if your corners don’t meet, the whole quilt tells on you. But I don’t know that I think something terrible will happen.\n\nIt’s more like… I get caught up in how it ought to be, and then I start fussing over the design or the schedule, and before I know it I’m talking about fabric choices when someone only asked me what time the meeting starts. So perfection, maybe, but not in a “bad thing will happen” way. More like I lose the thread a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to be in charge of situations to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s being in charge exactly. I don’t sit there thinking, “I have to run this or I won’t be comfortable.” It’s more that if nobody seems to know what’s happening, I start filling in the blanks.\n\nLike with a sewing circle, if the thread and scissors and patterns are all scattered around, someone has to put them in order—or at least that’s what my mind does. My aunt used to have little tins for every color of button, and I always admired that. But then I suppose I can get carried away and start deciding where everyone’s buttons ought to go, even if they didn’t ask me.\n\nSo… maybe I feel better when there’s a plan. I’m not sure I’d say I need to be in charge, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your tendency to take on too much and then struggle is causing you a lot of stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I suppose it is causing problems. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Maybe… I mean, if someone else were really in charge, maybe I’d tell myself I didn’t have to do quite so much. But then I think of the historical society fundraiser, and I just sort of stepped into it like I could run the whole thing myself.

I was starting quilt designs, calling volunteers, making schedules, telling people things would be ready by certain dates… and honestly, those dates weren’t realistic. I think I just got swept up. My aunt used to say a quilt looks simple until you’re the one matching all the corners, and that’s sort of how it was. Before I knew it, I’d promised more than I could possibly get done.

I was acting like I could run the whole thing myself—making quilt designs, calling all these volunteers, drawing up schedules, promising people deadlines. And then, of course, it was way more than one person could do.\n\nI used to help with sewing projects years ago, my aunt had this old quilting frame in her dining room, and I kept thinking, “Oh, I know how this should go.” But I got carried away. I just kept adding more and more, and now I’ve made a bit of a mess of it.\n\nDoctor: Has this kind of thing, where you take on too much and then struggle, happened to you before?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… this fundraiser is just the clearest example because it’s all out in public now. I started with just helping a little, and then suddenly I was doing quilt patterns, calling people, making schedules, telling everyone when things would be done—like I was in charge of the whole thing.\n\nI’ve had that sort of thing happen before, where I get very sure I can handle everything. It reminds me of my aunt’s quilting frame, actually—she could keep a whole room of women organized without even raising her voice. I suppose I thought I could do that too. But then I promise too much, and it turns into more than I can really manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this pattern of taking on too much and then struggling is something that happens in other areas of your life, not just with the historical society?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose it does, though the historical society one is the one everyone can point to. I started out just helping, and then I was making quilt designs, calling volunteers, writing schedules, promising deadlines—like I could run the whole fundraiser myself.\n\nAnd quilting is funny, you know, because once you start laying out the pieces, you think, “Oh, I can see the whole pattern,” but then there are all these little seams and corners you didn’t account for. That’s sort of how I get. I see the big plan and jump in, and then suddenly I’ve said yes to far more than I can actually finish.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to do things perfectly, or that if you don't, something bad will happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I do like things to look right, especially with sewing—my aunt always said if your corners don’t meet, the whole quilt tells on you. But I don’t know that I think something terrible will happen.\n\nIt’s more like… I get caught up in how it ought to be, and then I start fussing over the design or the schedule, and before I know it I’m talking about fabric choices when someone only asked me what time the meeting starts. So perfection, maybe, but not in a “bad thing will happen” way. More like I lose the thread a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to be in charge of situations to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s being in charge exactly. I don’t sit there thinking, “I have to run this or I won’t be comfortable.” It’s more that if nobody seems to know what’s happening, I start filling in the blanks.\n\nLike with a sewing circle, if the thread and scissors and patterns are all scattered around, someone has to put them in order—or at least that’s what my mind does. My aunt used to have little tins for every color of button, and I always admired that. But then I suppose I can get carried away and start deciding where everyone’s buttons ought to go, even if they didn’t ask me.\n\nSo… maybe I feel better when there’s a plan. I’m not sure I’d say I need to be in charge, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your tendency to take on too much and then struggle is causing you a lot of stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I suppose it is causing problems. More than I like to admit.\n\nMy husband has had to pick up a lot of the house routines lately—things I always used to keep track of, like what needs doing and who’s supposed to be where, that sort of thing. But then I suppose I can get carried away and start deciding where everyone’s buttons ought to go, even if they didn’t ask me.\n\nSo… maybe I feel better when there’s a plan. I’m not sure I’d say I need to be in charge, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your tendency to take on too much and then struggle is causing you a lot of stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I suppose it is causing problems. More than I like to admit.\n\nMy husband has had to pick up a lot of the house routines lately—things I always used to keep track of, like what needs doing and who’s supposed to be where, that sort of thing. And with the historical society… well, he’s had to deal with some of that too, because I stopped keeping up with what I’d promised. I just couldn’t face people waiting on me, asking where things were.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, really. I start out thinking I’m helping, and then it turns into this big tangled spool of thread, and suddenly I don’t want to answer the phone.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I worry about things, but I don’t know if it’s more than anybody else would. There’s always something to think about, isn’t there? Bills, family, whether I remembered to put the good scissors back where they belong—my aunt had a pair she’d practically guard with her life.\n\nBut anxious… I don’t know. Sometimes I get wound up when things feel unfinished, or when I think I’ve left someone waiting. Then again, I can spend half an hour fretting over a pattern piece and forget what I was upset about in the first place. So yes, maybe some worry, but I’m not sure how to measure “often.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often have recurring thoughts or ideas that you feel compelled to act on?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I do get little ideas stuck in my head sometimes, like “I should sort that drawer” or “I need to call so-and-so,” but I don’t know that I feel compelled exactly.\n\nIt’s funny, because my aunt used to say if a thought keeps tapping at the window, you might as well let it in—but she was talking about quilting patterns, not anything serious. Sometimes I’ll start with one thought and then it turns into three other things, and before I know it I’m standing in the pantry wondering why I came in there.\n\nSo… recurring thoughts, maybe in the ordinary sense. But feeling forced to act on them? I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed anything like that in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your tendency to take on too much and then struggle is more about wanting everything to be perfect or more about wanting to be in control of the situation?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m not sure it’s either of those exactly. Perfect, control—those both sound a bit stronger than how it feels in my head.\n\nI think sometimes I just get carried along by the idea of doing something useful, or not disappointing someone. And then, well, once you’ve said yes, it’s like when you start pulling one thread and the whole hem comes loose. My mother used to say you should never agree to alter a dress before you’ve looked at the seams properly, and I suppose that applies to other things too.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s perfection or control. I haven’t really thought of it that way. It just feels like I mean well, and then I’ve made a mess of too many promises.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still take on so many tasks if you knew someone else was in charge and would handle any mistakes that might happen?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I mean, if someone else were really in charge, maybe I’d tell myself I didn’t have to do quite so much. But then I think of the historical society fundraiser, and I just sort of stepped into it like I could run the whole thing myself.\n\nI was starting quilt designs, calling volunteers, making schedules, telling people things would be ready by certain dates… and honestly, those dates weren’t realistic. I think I just got swept up. My aunt used to say a quilt looks simple until you’re the one matching all the corners, and that’s sort of how it was. Before I knew it, I’d promised more than I could possibly get done.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I suppose… yes, maybe I would feel less driven if it truly wasn’t on me. But with the historical society fundraiser, I acted like the whole thing was mine to carry.

I started making quilt designs, calling volunteers, putting together schedules, telling people we’d have things done by certain dates—and then, well, those dates were not realistic. It was like I’d decided I could run the whole fundraiser myself, the way my aunt would take over a church supper and suddenly be stirring three pots at once.

So I don’t know if “responsible” is exactly the word, but once I felt like it was mine, I just kept adding more and more.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what finally made me come in is this thing with the historical society. I sort of… took over the fundraiser, or tried to. I was acting like I could run the whole thing myself—making quilt designs, calling all these volunteers, drawing up schedules, promising people deadlines. And then, of course, it was way more than one person could do.\n\nI used to help with sewing projects years ago, my aunt had this old quilting frame in her dining room, and I kept thinking, “Oh, I know how this should go.” But I got carried away. I just kept adding more and more, and now I’ve made a bit of a mess of it.\n\nDoctor: Has this kind of thing, where you take on too much and then struggle, happened to you before?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… this fundraiser is just the clearest example because it’s all out in public now. I started with just helping a little, and then suddenly I was doing quilt patterns, calling people, making schedules, telling everyone when things would be done—like I was in charge of the whole thing.\n\nI’ve had that sort of thing happen before, where I get very sure I can handle everything. It reminds me of my aunt’s quilting frame, actually—she could keep a whole room of women organized without even raising her voice. I suppose I thought I could do that too. But then I promise too much, and it turns into more than I can really manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this pattern of taking on too much and then struggling is something that happens in other areas of your life, not just with the historical society?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose it does, though the historical society one is the one everyone can point to. I started out just helping, and then I was making quilt designs, calling volunteers, writing schedules, promising deadlines—like I could run the whole fundraiser myself.\n\nAnd quilting is funny, you know, because once you start laying out the pieces, you think, “Oh, I can see the whole pattern,” but then there are all these little seams and corners you didn’t account for. That’s sort of how I get. I see the big plan and jump in, and then suddenly I’ve said yes to far more than I can actually finish.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to do things perfectly, or that if you don't, something bad will happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I do like things to look right, especially with sewing—my aunt always said if your corners don’t meet, the whole quilt tells on you. But I don’t know that I think something terrible will happen.\n\nIt’s more like… I get caught up in how it ought to be, and then I start fussing over the design or the schedule, and before I know it I’m talking about fabric choices when someone only asked me what time the meeting starts. So perfection, maybe, but not in a “bad thing will happen” way. More like I lose the thread a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to be in charge of situations to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s being in charge exactly. I don’t sit there thinking, “I have to run this or I won’t be comfortable.” It’s more that if nobody seems to know what’s happening, I start filling in the blanks.\n\nLike with a sewing circle, if the thread and scissors and patterns are all scattered around, someone has to put them in order—or at least that’s what my mind does. My aunt used to have little tins for every color of button, and I always admired that. But then I suppose I can get carried away and start deciding where everyone’s buttons ought to go, even if they didn’t ask me.\n\nSo… maybe I feel better when there’s a plan. I’m not sure I’d say I need to be in charge, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your tendency to take on too much and then struggle is causing you a lot of stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I suppose it is causing problems. More than I like to admit.\n\nMy husband has had to pick up a lot of the house routines lately—things I always used to keep track of, like what needs doing and who’s supposed to be where, that sort of thing. And with the historical society… well, he’s had to deal with some of that too, because I stopped keeping up with what I’d promised. I just couldn’t face people waiting on me, asking where things were.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, really. I start out thinking I’m helping, and then it turns into this big tangled spool of thread, and suddenly I don’t want to answer the phone.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I worry about things, but I don’t know if it’s more than anybody else would. There’s always something to think about, isn’t there? Bills, family, whether I remembered to put the good scissors back where they belong—my aunt had a pair she’d practically guard with her life.\n\nBut anxious… I don’t know. Sometimes I get wound up when things feel unfinished, or when I think I’ve left someone waiting. Then again, I can spend half an hour fretting over a pattern piece and forget what I was upset about in the first place. So yes, maybe some worry, but I’m not sure how to measure “often.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often have recurring thoughts or ideas that you feel compelled to act on?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I do get little ideas stuck in my head sometimes, like “I should sort that drawer” or “I need to call so-and-so,” but I don’t know that I feel compelled exactly.\n\nIt’s funny, because my aunt used to say if a thought keeps tapping at the window, you might as well let it in—but she was talking about quilting patterns, not anything serious. Sometimes I’ll start with one thought and then it turns into three other things, and before I know it I’m standing in the pantry wondering why I came in there.\n\nSo… recurring thoughts, maybe in the ordinary sense. But feeling forced to act on them? I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed anything like that in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your tendency to take on too much and then struggle is more about wanting everything to be perfect or more about wanting to be in control of the situation?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m not sure it’s either of those exactly. Perfect, control—those both sound a bit stronger than how it feels in my head.\n\nI think sometimes I just get carried along by the idea of doing something useful, or not disappointing someone. And then, well, once you’ve said yes, it’s like when you start pulling one thread and the whole hem comes loose. My mother used to say you should never agree to alter a dress before you’ve looked at the seams properly, and I suppose that applies to other things too.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s perfection or control. I haven’t really thought of it that way. It just feels like I mean well, and then I’ve made a mess of too many promises.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still take on so many tasks if you knew someone else was in charge and would handle any mistakes that might happen?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I mean, if someone else were really in charge, maybe I’d tell myself I didn’t have to do quite so much. But then I think of the historical society fundraiser, and I just sort of stepped into it like I could run the whole thing myself.\n\nI was starting quilt designs, calling volunteers, making schedules, telling people things would be ready by certain dates… and honestly, those dates weren’t realistic. I think I just got swept up. My aunt used to say a quilt looks simple until you’re the one matching all the corners, and that’s sort of how it was. Before I knew it, I’d promised more than I could possibly get done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel less motivated to take on tasks if you didn't feel responsible for their outcome?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, maybe I would feel less driven if it truly wasn’t on me. But with the historical society fundraiser, I acted like the whole thing was mine to carry.\n\nI started making quilt designs, calling volunteers, putting together schedules, telling people we’d have things done by certain dates—and then, well, those dates were not realistic. It was like I’d decided I could run the whole fundraiser myself, the way my aunt would take over a church supper and suddenly be stirring three pots at once.\n\nSo I don’t know if “responsible” is exactly the word, but once I felt like it was mine, I just kept adding more and more.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t know that I’d call it a strong need. I like things to be findable, I suppose—scissors in the drawer, thread where it belongs—but then everybody likes that, don’t they?

Though my sewing basket is never as neat as my mother’s was. She had these little tins for buttons, all sorted by color, and I used to think that was just the height of civilization. Mine starts that way and then somehow there are receipts and cough drops in there too.

So… organized and in their place? I’m not sure. I notice when things are a mess, but I don’t know if I need it that way.

I haven’t really thought of it that way. It just feels like I mean well, and then I’ve made a mess of too many promises.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still take on so many tasks if you knew someone else was in charge and would handle any mistakes that might happen?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I mean, if someone else were really in charge, maybe I’d tell myself I didn’t have to do quite so much. I think I just got swept up. My aunt used to say a quilt looks simple until you’re the one matching all the corners, and that’s sort of how it was. Before I knew it, I’d promised more than I could possibly get done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel less motivated to take on tasks if you didn't feel responsible for their outcome?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, maybe I would feel less driven if it truly wasn’t on me. Maybe… normally I might step back if someone was clearly better at something. I mean, I know when someone else can bake a pie better than me—my cousin could make a crust that practically sang, and mine always shrinks in the pan.

It reminds me of my aunt’s quilting frame, actually—she could keep a whole room of women organized without even raising her voice. I suppose I thought I could do that too. But then I promise too much, and it turns into more than I can really manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this pattern of taking on too much and then struggling is something that happens in other areas of your life, not just with the historical society?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose it does, though the historical society one is the one everyone can point to. I started out just helping, and then I was making quilt designs, calling volunteers, writing schedules, promising deadlines—like I could run the whole fundraiser myself.\n\nAnd quilting is funny, you know, because once you start laying out the pieces, you think, “Oh, I can see the whole pattern,” but then there are all these little seams and corners you didn’t account for. That’s sort of how I get. I see the big plan and jump in, and then suddenly I’ve said yes to far more than I can actually finish.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to do things perfectly, or that if you don't, something bad will happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I do like things to look right, especially with sewing—my aunt always said if your corners don’t meet, the whole quilt tells on you. But I don’t know that I think something terrible will happen.\n\nIt’s more like… I get caught up in how it ought to be, and then I start fussing over the design or the schedule, and before I know it I’m talking about fabric choices when someone only asked me what time the meeting starts. So perfection, maybe, but not in a “bad thing will happen” way. More like I lose the thread a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to be in charge of situations to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s being in charge exactly. I don’t sit there thinking, “I have to run this or I won’t be comfortable.” It’s more that if nobody seems to know what’s happening, I start filling in the blanks.\n\nLike with a sewing circle, if the thread and scissors and patterns are all scattered around, someone has to put them in order—or at least that’s what my mind does. My aunt used to have little tins for every color of button, and I always admired that. But then I suppose I can get carried away and start deciding where everyone’s buttons ought to go, even if they didn’t ask me.\n\nSo… maybe I feel better when there’s a plan. I’m not sure I’d say I need to be in charge, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your tendency to take on too much and then struggle is causing you a lot of stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I suppose it is causing problems. More than I like to admit.\n\nMy husband has had to pick up a lot of the house routines lately—things I always used to keep track of, like what needs doing and who’s supposed to be where, that sort of thing. And with the historical society… well, he’s had to deal with some of that too, because I stopped keeping up with what I’d promised. I just couldn’t face people waiting on me, asking where things were.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, really. I start out thinking I’m helping, and then it turns into this big tangled spool of thread, and suddenly I don’t want to answer the phone.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I worry about things, but I don’t know if it’s more than anybody else would. There’s always something to think about, isn’t there? Bills, family, whether I remembered to put the good scissors back where they belong—my aunt had a pair she’d practically guard with her life.\n\nBut anxious… I don’t know. Sometimes I get wound up when things feel unfinished, or when I think I’ve left someone waiting. Then again, I can spend half an hour fretting over a pattern piece and forget what I was upset about in the first place. So yes, maybe some worry, but I’m not sure how to measure “often.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often have recurring thoughts or ideas that you feel compelled to act on?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I do get little ideas stuck in my head sometimes, like “I should sort that drawer” or “I need to call so-and-so,” but I don’t know that I feel compelled exactly.\n\nIt’s funny, because my aunt used to say if a thought keeps tapping at the window, you might as well let it in—but she was talking about quilting patterns, not anything serious. Sometimes I’ll start with one thought and then it turns into three other things, and before I know it I’m standing in the pantry wondering why I came in there.\n\nSo… recurring thoughts, maybe in the ordinary sense. But feeling forced to act on them? I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed anything like that in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your tendency to take on too much and then struggle is more about wanting everything to be perfect or more about wanting to be in control of the situation?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m not sure it’s either of those exactly. Perfect, control—those both sound a bit stronger than how it feels in my head.\n\nI think sometimes I just get carried along by the idea of doing something useful, or not disappointing someone. And then, well, once you’ve said yes, it’s like when you start pulling one thread and the whole hem comes loose. My mother used to say you should never agree to alter a dress before you’ve looked at the seams properly, and I suppose that applies to other things too.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s perfection or control. I haven’t really thought of it that way. It just feels like I mean well, and then I’ve made a mess of too many promises.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still take on so many tasks if you knew someone else was in charge and would handle any mistakes that might happen?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I mean, if someone else were really in charge, maybe I’d tell myself I didn’t have to do quite so much. But then I think of the historical society fundraiser, and I just sort of stepped into it like I could run the whole thing myself.\n\nI was starting quilt designs, calling volunteers, making schedules, telling people things would be ready by certain dates… and honestly, those dates weren’t realistic. I think I just got swept up. My aunt used to say a quilt looks simple until you’re the one matching all the corners, and that’s sort of how it was. Before I knew it, I’d promised more than I could possibly get done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel less motivated to take on tasks if you didn't feel responsible for their outcome?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, maybe I would feel less driven if it truly wasn’t on me. But with the historical society fundraiser, I acted like the whole thing was mine to carry.\n\nI started making quilt designs, calling volunteers, putting together schedules, telling people we’d have things done by certain dates—and then, well, those dates were not realistic. It was like I’d decided I could run the whole fundraiser myself, the way my aunt would take over a church supper and suddenly be stirring three pots at once.\n\nSo I don’t know if “responsible” is exactly the word, but once I felt like it was mine, I just kept adding more and more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to have things organized and in their place?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a strong need. I like things to be findable, I suppose—scissors in the drawer, thread where it belongs—but then everybody likes that, don’t they?\n\nThough my sewing basket is never as neat as my mother’s was. She had these little tins for buttons, all sorted by color, and I used to think that was just the height of civilization. Mine starts that way and then somehow there are receipts and cough drops in there too.\n\nSo… organized and in their place? I’m not sure. I notice when things are a mess, but I don’t know if I need it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be less likely to take on a task if you thought someone else could do it better than you?\n\nPatient: Maybe… normally I might step back if someone was clearly better at something. I mean, I know when someone else can bake a pie better than me—my cousin could make a crust that practically sang, and mine always shrinks in the pan.\n\nBut with the heritage quilts, no, I didn’t really think that way. I truly believed my ideas were going to put our little town on the map, like I’d finally found the one thing only I could make happen. It sounds a little grand saying it now, but at the time it felt obvious to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates": ["F60.8", "F34.0", "F48.8"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

I mean, I know when someone else can bake a pie better than me—my cousin could make a crust that practically sang, and mine always shrinks in the pan.\n\nBut with the heritage quilts, no, I didn’t really think that way. I truly believed my ideas were going to put our little town on the map, like I’d finally found the one thing only I could make happen. It sounds a little grand saying it now, but at the time it felt obvious to me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would be less likely to take on tasks if you weren't afraid of them not turning out well?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 59 / not_in_workforce / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’ve thought about it that way. I mean, I do worry how things will turn out sometimes, sure, but whether that’s what makes me take something on or not… I don’t know.

With some things I just get it in my head that it needs doing, and then I’m already halfway into it before I’ve really asked myself why. Like when you start cleaning one cupboard and suddenly all the dishes are on the floor and you find that chipped mug from the county fair—then you’re stuck finishing, aren’t you?

So… if I wasn’t afraid it wouldn’t turn out well, would I take on less? I don’t really know. That’s a hard one to separate out.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF60.8, F34.0, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what finally made me come in is this thing with the historical society. I sort of… took over the fundraiser, or tried to. I was acting like I could run the whole thing myself—making quilt designs, calling all these volunteers, drawing up schedules, promising people deadlines. And then, of course, it was way more than one person could do.\n\nI used to help with sewing projects years ago, my aunt had this old quilting frame in her dining room, and I kept thinking, “Oh, I know how this should go.” But I got carried away. I just kept adding more and more, and now I’ve made a bit of a mess of it.\n\nDoctor: Has this kind of thing, where you take on too much and then struggle, happened to you before?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… this fundraiser is just the clearest example because it’s all out in public now. I started with just helping a little, and then suddenly I was doing quilt patterns, calling people, making schedules, telling everyone when things would be done—like I was in charge of the whole thing.\n\nI’ve had that sort of thing happen before, where I get very sure I can handle everything. It reminds me of my aunt’s quilting frame, actually—she could keep a whole room of women organized without even raising her voice. I suppose I thought I could do that too. But then I promise too much, and it turns into more than I can really manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this pattern of taking on too much and then struggling is something that happens in other areas of your life, not just with the historical society?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose it does, though the historical society one is the one everyone can point to. I started out just helping, and then I was making quilt designs, calling volunteers, writing schedules, promising deadlines—like I could run the whole fundraiser myself.\n\nAnd quilting is funny, you know, because once you start laying out the pieces, you think, “Oh, I can see the whole pattern,” but then there are all these little seams and corners you didn’t account for. That’s sort of how I get. I see the big plan and jump in, and then suddenly I’ve said yes to far more than I can actually finish.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to do things perfectly, or that if you don't, something bad will happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I do like things to look right, especially with sewing—my aunt always said if your corners don’t meet, the whole quilt tells on you. But I don’t know that I think something terrible will happen.\n\nIt’s more like… I get caught up in how it ought to be, and then I start fussing over the design or the schedule, and before I know it I’m talking about fabric choices when someone only asked me what time the meeting starts. So perfection, maybe, but not in a “bad thing will happen” way. More like I lose the thread a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to be in charge of situations to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s being in charge exactly. I don’t sit there thinking, “I have to run this or I won’t be comfortable.” It’s more that if nobody seems to know what’s happening, I start filling in the blanks.\n\nLike with a sewing circle, if the thread and scissors and patterns are all scattered around, someone has to put them in order—or at least that’s what my mind does. My aunt used to have little tins for every color of button, and I always admired that. But then I suppose I can get carried away and start deciding where everyone’s buttons ought to go, even if they didn’t ask me.\n\nSo… maybe I feel better when there’s a plan. I’m not sure I’d say I need to be in charge, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your tendency to take on too much and then struggle is causing you a lot of stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I suppose it is causing problems. More than I like to admit.\n\nMy husband has had to pick up a lot of the house routines lately—things I always used to keep track of, like what needs doing and who’s supposed to be where, that sort of thing. And with the historical society… well, he’s had to deal with some of that too, because I stopped keeping up with what I’d promised. I just couldn’t face people waiting on me, asking where things were.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, really. I start out thinking I’m helping, and then it turns into this big tangled spool of thread, and suddenly I don’t want to answer the phone.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I worry about things, but I don’t know if it’s more than anybody else would. There’s always something to think about, isn’t there? Bills, family, whether I remembered to put the good scissors back where they belong—my aunt had a pair she’d practically guard with her life.\n\nBut anxious… I don’t know. Sometimes I get wound up when things feel unfinished, or when I think I’ve left someone waiting. Then again, I can spend half an hour fretting over a pattern piece and forget what I was upset about in the first place. So yes, maybe some worry, but I’m not sure how to measure “often.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often have recurring thoughts or ideas that you feel compelled to act on?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I do get little ideas stuck in my head sometimes, like “I should sort that drawer” or “I need to call so-and-so,” but I don’t know that I feel compelled exactly.\n\nIt’s funny, because my aunt used to say if a thought keeps tapping at the window, you might as well let it in—but she was talking about quilting patterns, not anything serious. Sometimes I’ll start with one thought and then it turns into three other things, and before I know it I’m standing in the pantry wondering why I came in there.\n\nSo… recurring thoughts, maybe in the ordinary sense. But feeling forced to act on them? I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed anything like that in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your tendency to take on too much and then struggle is more about wanting everything to be perfect or more about wanting to be in control of the situation?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m not sure it’s either of those exactly. Perfect, control—those both sound a bit stronger than how it feels in my head.\n\nI think sometimes I just get carried along by the idea of doing something useful, or not disappointing someone. And then, well, once you’ve said yes, it’s like when you start pulling one thread and the whole hem comes loose. My mother used to say you should never agree to alter a dress before you’ve looked at the seams properly, and I suppose that applies to other things too.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s perfection or control. I haven’t really thought of it that way. It just feels like I mean well, and then I’ve made a mess of too many promises.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still take on so many tasks if you knew someone else was in charge and would handle any mistakes that might happen?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I mean, if someone else were really in charge, maybe I’d tell myself I didn’t have to do quite so much. But then I think of the historical society fundraiser, and I just sort of stepped into it like I could run the whole thing myself.\n\nI was starting quilt designs, calling volunteers, making schedules, telling people things would be ready by certain dates… and honestly, those dates weren’t realistic. I think I just got swept up. My aunt used to say a quilt looks simple until you’re the one matching all the corners, and that’s sort of how it was. Before I knew it, I’d promised more than I could possibly get done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel less motivated to take on tasks if you didn't feel responsible for their outcome?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, maybe I would feel less driven if it truly wasn’t on me. But with the historical society fundraiser, I acted like the whole thing was mine to carry.\n\nI started making quilt designs, calling volunteers, putting together schedules, telling people we’d have things done by certain dates—and then, well, those dates were not realistic. It was like I’d decided I could run the whole fundraiser myself, the way my aunt would take over a church supper and suddenly be stirring three pots at once.\n\nSo I don’t know if “responsible” is exactly the word, but once I felt like it was mine, I just kept adding more and more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to have things organized and in their place?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a strong need. I like things to be findable, I suppose—scissors in the drawer, thread where it belongs—but then everybody likes that, don’t they?\n\nThough my sewing basket is never as neat as my mother’s was. She had these little tins for buttons, all sorted by color, and I used to think that was just the height of civilization. Mine starts that way and then somehow there are receipts and cough drops in there too.\n\nSo… organized and in their place? I’m not sure. I notice when things are a mess, but I don’t know if I need it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be less likely to take on a task if you thought someone else could do it better than you?\n\nPatient: Maybe… normally I might step back if someone was clearly better at something. I mean, I know when someone else can bake a pie better than me—my cousin could make a crust that practically sang, and mine always shrinks in the pan.\n\nBut with the heritage quilts, no, I didn’t really think that way. I truly believed my ideas were going to put our little town on the map, like I’d finally found the one thing only I could make happen. It sounds a little grand saying it now, but at the time it felt obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be less likely to take on tasks if you weren't afraid of them not turning out well?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve thought about it that way. I mean, I do worry how things will turn out sometimes, sure, but whether that’s what makes me take something on or not… I don’t know.\n\nWith some things I just get it in my head that it needs doing, and then I’m already halfway into it before I’ve really asked myself why. Like when you start cleaning one cupboard and suddenly all the dishes are on the floor and you find that chipped mug from the county fair—then you’re stuck finishing, aren’t you?\n\nSo… if I wasn’t afraid it wouldn’t turn out well, would I take on less? I don’t really know. That’s a hard one to separate out.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I truly believed my ideas were going to put our little town on the map, like I’d finally found the one thing only I could make happen. It sounds a little grand saying it now, but at the time it felt obvious to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be less likely to take on tasks if you weren't afraid of them not turning out well?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve thought about it that way. I mean, I do worry how things will turn out sometimes, sure, but whether that’s what makes me take something on or not… I don’t know.\n\nWith some things I just get it in my head that it needs doing, and then I’m already halfway into it before I’ve really asked myself why. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure it’s fear, exactly. Maybe sometimes, but I don’t notice myself thinking, “Oh, I’d better do this or it’ll be ruined.” It’s more like… I get a picture in my head of how it should go, and then I start fussing with all the pieces.

But “control” sounds so harsh, doesn’t it? My mother used to say my aunt controlled Thanksgiving because she had a certain way the gravy boat had to be placed, and honestly the gravy was the least of everyone’s problems that year because the oven door wouldn’t shut right.

So I don’t know. I may take over more than I realize, but whether that’s because I’m afraid of doing it badly… I can’t really say.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF60.8, F34.0, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what finally made me come in is this thing with the historical society. I sort of… took over the fundraiser, or tried to. I was acting like I could run the whole thing myself—making quilt designs, calling all these volunteers, drawing up schedules, promising people deadlines. And then, of course, it was way more than one person could do.\n\nI used to help with sewing projects years ago, my aunt had this old quilting frame in her dining room, and I kept thinking, “Oh, I know how this should go.” But I got carried away. I just kept adding more and more, and now I’ve made a bit of a mess of it.\n\nDoctor: Has this kind of thing, where you take on too much and then struggle, happened to you before?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… this fundraiser is just the clearest example because it’s all out in public now. I started with just helping a little, and then suddenly I was doing quilt patterns, calling people, making schedules, telling everyone when things would be done—like I was in charge of the whole thing.\n\nI’ve had that sort of thing happen before, where I get very sure I can handle everything. It reminds me of my aunt’s quilting frame, actually—she could keep a whole room of women organized without even raising her voice. I suppose I thought I could do that too. But then I promise too much, and it turns into more than I can really manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this pattern of taking on too much and then struggling is something that happens in other areas of your life, not just with the historical society?\n\nPatient: Well… yes, I suppose it does, though the historical society one is the one everyone can point to. I started out just helping, and then I was making quilt designs, calling volunteers, writing schedules, promising deadlines—like I could run the whole fundraiser myself.\n\nAnd quilting is funny, you know, because once you start laying out the pieces, you think, “Oh, I can see the whole pattern,” but then there are all these little seams and corners you didn’t account for. That’s sort of how I get. I see the big plan and jump in, and then suddenly I’ve said yes to far more than I can actually finish.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to do things perfectly, or that if you don't, something bad will happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I do like things to look right, especially with sewing—my aunt always said if your corners don’t meet, the whole quilt tells on you. But I don’t know that I think something terrible will happen.\n\nIt’s more like… I get caught up in how it ought to be, and then I start fussing over the design or the schedule, and before I know it I’m talking about fabric choices when someone only asked me what time the meeting starts. So perfection, maybe, but not in a “bad thing will happen” way. More like I lose the thread a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to be in charge of situations to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s being in charge exactly. I don’t sit there thinking, “I have to run this or I won’t be comfortable.” It’s more that if nobody seems to know what’s happening, I start filling in the blanks.\n\nLike with a sewing circle, if the thread and scissors and patterns are all scattered around, someone has to put them in order—or at least that’s what my mind does. My aunt used to have little tins for every color of button, and I always admired that. But then I suppose I can get carried away and start deciding where everyone’s buttons ought to go, even if they didn’t ask me.\n\nSo… maybe I feel better when there’s a plan. I’m not sure I’d say I need to be in charge, though.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your tendency to take on too much and then struggle is causing you a lot of stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I suppose it is causing problems. More than I like to admit.\n\nMy husband has had to pick up a lot of the house routines lately—things I always used to keep track of, like what needs doing and who’s supposed to be where, that sort of thing. And with the historical society… well, he’s had to deal with some of that too, because I stopped keeping up with what I’d promised. I just couldn’t face people waiting on me, asking where things were.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, really. I start out thinking I’m helping, and then it turns into this big tangled spool of thread, and suddenly I don’t want to answer the phone.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or worried about things?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I worry about things, but I don’t know if it’s more than anybody else would. There’s always something to think about, isn’t there? Bills, family, whether I remembered to put the good scissors back where they belong—my aunt had a pair she’d practically guard with her life.\n\nBut anxious… I don’t know. Sometimes I get wound up when things feel unfinished, or when I think I’ve left someone waiting. Then again, I can spend half an hour fretting over a pattern piece and forget what I was upset about in the first place. So yes, maybe some worry, but I’m not sure how to measure “often.”\n\nDoctor: Do you often have recurring thoughts or ideas that you feel compelled to act on?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I do get little ideas stuck in my head sometimes, like “I should sort that drawer” or “I need to call so-and-so,” but I don’t know that I feel compelled exactly.\n\nIt’s funny, because my aunt used to say if a thought keeps tapping at the window, you might as well let it in—but she was talking about quilting patterns, not anything serious. Sometimes I’ll start with one thought and then it turns into three other things, and before I know it I’m standing in the pantry wondering why I came in there.\n\nSo… recurring thoughts, maybe in the ordinary sense. But feeling forced to act on them? I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed anything like that in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your tendency to take on too much and then struggle is more about wanting everything to be perfect or more about wanting to be in control of the situation?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I’m not sure it’s either of those exactly. Perfect, control—those both sound a bit stronger than how it feels in my head.\n\nI think sometimes I just get carried along by the idea of doing something useful, or not disappointing someone. And then, well, once you’ve said yes, it’s like when you start pulling one thread and the whole hem comes loose. My mother used to say you should never agree to alter a dress before you’ve looked at the seams properly, and I suppose that applies to other things too.\n\nSo… I don’t know if it’s perfection or control. I haven’t really thought of it that way. It just feels like I mean well, and then I’ve made a mess of too many promises.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still take on so many tasks if you knew someone else was in charge and would handle any mistakes that might happen?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I mean, if someone else were really in charge, maybe I’d tell myself I didn’t have to do quite so much. But then I think of the historical society fundraiser, and I just sort of stepped into it like I could run the whole thing myself.\n\nI was starting quilt designs, calling volunteers, making schedules, telling people things would be ready by certain dates… and honestly, those dates weren’t realistic. I think I just got swept up. My aunt used to say a quilt looks simple until you’re the one matching all the corners, and that’s sort of how it was. Before I knew it, I’d promised more than I could possibly get done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel less motivated to take on tasks if you didn't feel responsible for their outcome?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, maybe I would feel less driven if it truly wasn’t on me. But with the historical society fundraiser, I acted like the whole thing was mine to carry.\n\nI started making quilt designs, calling volunteers, putting together schedules, telling people we’d have things done by certain dates—and then, well, those dates were not realistic. It was like I’d decided I could run the whole fundraiser myself, the way my aunt would take over a church supper and suddenly be stirring three pots at once.\n\nSo I don’t know if “responsible” is exactly the word, but once I felt like it was mine, I just kept adding more and more.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to have things organized and in their place?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a strong need. I like things to be findable, I suppose—scissors in the drawer, thread where it belongs—but then everybody likes that, don’t they?\n\nThough my sewing basket is never as neat as my mother’s was. Mine starts that way and then somehow there are receipts and cough drops in there too.\n\nSo… organized and in their place? I’m not sure. I notice when things are a mess, but I don’t know if I need it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be less likely to take on a task if you thought someone else could do it better than you?\n\nPatient: Maybe… normally I might step back if someone was clearly better at something. I mean, I know when someone else can bake a pie better than me—my cousin could make a crust that practically sang, and mine always shrinks in the pan.\n\nBut with the heritage quilts, no, I didn’t really think that way.
